People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Um… I guess I’m here because something’s been happening that I’m not handling as well as I’d like. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice — very clearly — like he’s right behind me when I’m out on the trail, or sometimes when I’m in my painting room.

I know that probably sounds… concerning. Part of me knows he’s dead, I do know that. But in the moment it feels completely real, like he’s actually there. I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing I should come in for, but… it’s been hard to ignore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because something’s been happening that I’m not handling as well as I’d like. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice — very clearly — like he’s right behind me when I’m out on the trail, or sometimes when I’m in my painting room.\n\nI know that probably sounds… concerning. Part of me knows he’s dead, I do know that. But in the moment it feels completely real, like he’s actually there. I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing I should come in for, but… it’s been hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it always Garrett's voice that you hear?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean… I don’t want to overstate it, but when it happens, it’s Garrett’s voice. It’s very clear, like he’s standing right behind me on the trail, or in my painting room. It’s more that I hear him so clearly, like he’s standing right behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room. And for a second it feels… real. I know that probably sounds strange, and I do know he’s dead, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like just a memory.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I guess. Since Garrett died. I mean, not every second or anything, but this whole thing has sort of been unfolding over the past month. How do you usually mean “most of the time”?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, of course I’ve been sad—Garrett died, so it would be strange if I wasn’t. How do you usually mean “most of the time”?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Um… pretty much since Garrett passed, I think. At first I told myself it was just normal guilt, you know, going over things after someone dies.

But it’s been… persistent. I keep replaying every last conversation I had with him, and then it spreads to other things too—like patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed everyone somehow.

I know that may sound excessive. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It just… keeps coming back, this sense that I’m in the way, and that people might be better off if I weren’t. Is that what you mean by worthlessness?

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because something’s been happening that I’m not handling as well as I’d like. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice — very clearly — like he’s right behind me when I’m out on the trail, or sometimes when I’m in my painting room.\n\nI know that probably sounds… concerning. Part of me knows he’s dead, I do know that. But in the moment it feels completely real, like he’s actually there. I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing I should come in for, but… it’s been hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Is it always Garrett's voice that you hear?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean… I don’t want to overstate it, but when it happens, it’s Garrett’s voice. It’s very clear, like he’s standing right behind me on the trail, or in my painting room. I know he’s dead — I do know that — but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you see or feel anything unusual when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see anything, no… at least not like an actual figure or anything. It’s more that I hear him so clearly, like he’s standing right behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room. And for a second it feels… real. I know that probably sounds strange, and I do know he’s dead, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like just a memory.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I guess. Since Garrett died. I mean, not every second or anything, but this whole thing has sort of been unfolding over the past month. Is that… the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does hearing Garrett's voice cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it is distressing. That’s really why I came in. When I hear Garrett’s voice, it’s so clear—like he’s right behind me on the trail or in my painting room—and even though part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it feels completely real. It scares me, honestly. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, of course I’ve been sad—Garrett died, so it would be strange if I wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m tracking it clearly enough to say it that way. I don’t want to overstate it. How do you usually mean “most of the time”?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still try to do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped functioning. But the things that usually keep me steady—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even sitting with patients—just feel… flat. Like I’m going through the motions, and I can’t really reach the part of me that used to care about them.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of “lost interest” you mean, but… yes, something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t want to say “hopeless” if that sounds too absolute. I can still imagine that things might get better, at least in theory.\n\nBut lately the future feels kind of… hard to picture, I guess. Like I’m just trying to get through the day without making too much of a mess of things. Does that count as hopeless, or is that different?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I’m not proud of that, and I don’t want it to sound dramatic.\n\nBut I keep going back over everything—every last conversation with Garrett, all the things I said or didn’t say. And then with work, the patient sessions I cancelled… I keep thinking I failed them too. Like I’m letting everyone down.\n\nSometimes it turns into this feeling that people would probably be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way. I know that may not be the right way to put it, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do.\n\nI know, logically, I may not have been able to change what happened, and I’m trying not to make it all about me. But I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett, looking for where I missed something or said the wrong thing, or didn’t do enough.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads to everything else—like the patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed him, and then I failed them too. It gets to this place where I feel like everyone would be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though my wife would probably describe it more clearly than I can.\n\nShe says sometimes I’m pacing around the house, moving papers from one place to another like I’m being driven by a motor. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and it’s like I’m slowed way down… she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me forever to answer.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yes, I think there’s been some of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it harder in a major way.\n\nI do find myself second-guessing things more, even small choices, but that may just be me being distracted or tired. I don’t want to overstate it. If you mean like being unable to make decisions at all, I don’t think it’s quite that… at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your physical activity level or movement since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my wife has noticed it more than I have.\n\nShe says I’ll pace around the house and keep rearranging papers, like I can’t settle down. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and everything seems slowed down—she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me a long time to answer.\n\nSo… yes, I guess there have been changes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or interest in since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean… I still try to do things, because I know I’m supposed to.\n\nPainting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater—those used to kind of anchor me. Even sitting with patients, honestly, used to feel meaningful. Lately it all just feels… flat. Like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but… no, not much really feels pleasurable right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… pretty much since Garrett passed, I think. At first I told myself it was just normal guilt, you know, going over things after someone dies.\n\nBut it’s been… persistent. I keep replaying every last conversation I had with him, and then it spreads to other things too—like patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed everyone somehow.\n\nI know that may sound excessive. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It just… keeps coming back, this sense that I’m in the way, and that people might be better off if I weren’t. Is that what you mean by worthlessness?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like I’m going through the motions, and I can’t really reach the part of me that used to care about them.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of “lost interest” you mean, but… yes, something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t want to say “hopeless” if that sounds too absolute. I can still imagine that things might get better, at least in theory.\n\nBut lately the future feels kind of… hard to picture, I guess. Like I’m just trying to get through the day without making too much of a mess of things. Does that count as hopeless, or is that different?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I’m not proud of that, and I don’t want it to sound dramatic.\n\nBut I keep going back over everything—every last conversation with Garrett, all the things I said or didn’t say. And then with work, the patient sessions I cancelled… I keep thinking I failed them too. Like I’m letting everyone down.\n\nSometimes it turns into this feeling that people would probably be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way. I know that may not be the right way to put it, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do.\n\nI know, logically, I may not have been able to change what happened, and I’m trying not to make it all about me. But I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett, looking for where I missed something or said the wrong thing, or didn’t do enough.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads to everything else—like the patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed him, and then I failed them too. It gets to this place where I feel like everyone would be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though my wife would probably describe it more clearly than I can.\n\nShe says sometimes I’m pacing around the house, moving papers from one place to another like I’m being driven by a motor. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and it’s like I’m slowed way down… she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me forever to answer.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yes, I think there’s been some of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it harder in a major way.\n\nI do find myself second-guessing things more, even small choices, but that may just be me being distracted or tired. I don’t want to overstate it. If you mean like being unable to make decisions at all, I don’t think it’s quite that… at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your physical activity level or movement since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my wife has noticed it more than I have.\n\nShe says I’ll pace around the house and keep rearranging papers, like I can’t settle down. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and everything seems slowed down—she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me a long time to answer.\n\nSo… yes, I guess there have been changes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or interest in since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. Even sitting with patients, honestly, used to feel meaningful. Lately it all just feels… flat. Like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but… no, not much really feels pleasurable right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… pretty much since Garrett passed, I think. At first I told myself it was just normal guilt, you know, going over things after someone dies.\n\nBut it’s been… persistent. I keep replaying every last conversation I had with him, and then it spreads to other things too—like patient sessions I cancelled. Is that what you mean by worthlessness?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other unusual perceptions, such as seeing things that aren't there, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Um… yes. I’ve been a little embarrassed to say it that way, because I know how it sounds.\n\nIt’s not so much seeing things, exactly. It’s more that I hear Garrett’s voice. I know that may not be the right way to put it, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do.\n\nI know, logically, I may not have been able to change what happened, and I’m trying not to make it all about me. But I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett, looking for where I missed something or said the wrong thing, or didn’t do enough.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads to everything else—like the patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed him, and then I failed them too. It gets to this place where I feel like everyone would be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though my wife would probably describe it more clearly than I can.\n\nShe says sometimes I’m pacing around the house, moving papers from one place to another like I’m being driven by a motor. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and it’s like I’m slowed way down… she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me forever to answer.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yes, I think there’s been some of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it harder in a major way.\n\nI do find myself second-guessing things more, even small choices, but that may just be me being distracted or tired. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s fair to say.

I might have tried to make it sound more manageable before, but the depressed, shut-down part has really been there most days for… about four months now. Most of the day, too, not just little moments.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because something’s been happening that I’m not handling as well as I’d like. I know he’s dead — I do know that — but in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you see or feel anything unusual when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see anything, no… at least not like an actual figure or anything. I know that probably sounds strange, and I do know he’s dead, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like just a memory.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I guess. Since Garrett died. I mean, not every second or anything, but this whole thing has sort of been unfolding over the past month. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, of course I’ve been sad—Garrett died, so it would be strange if I wasn’t. But I’m not sure I’m tracking it clearly enough to say it that way. I don’t want to overstate it. How do you usually mean “most of the time”?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still try to do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped functioning. But the things that usually keep me steady—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even sitting with patients—just feel… flat. Like I’m going through the motions, and I can’t really reach the part of me that used to care about them.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of “lost interest” you mean, but… yes, something like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I don’t want to say “hopeless” if that sounds too absolute. I can still imagine that things might get better, at least in theory.\n\nBut lately the future feels kind of… hard to picture, I guess. Like I’m just trying to get through the day without making too much of a mess of things. Does that count as hopeless, or is that different?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think so. I’m not proud of that, and I don’t want it to sound dramatic.\n\nBut I keep going back over everything—every last conversation with Garrett, all the things I said or didn’t say. And then with work, the patient sessions I cancelled… I keep thinking I failed them too. Like I’m letting everyone down.\n\nSometimes it turns into this feeling that people would probably be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way. I know that may not be the right way to put it, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do.\n\nI know, logically, I may not have been able to change what happened, and I’m trying not to make it all about me. But I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett, looking for where I missed something or said the wrong thing, or didn’t do enough.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads to everything else—like the patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed him, and then I failed them too. It gets to this place where I feel like everyone would be better off if I just… wasn’t in the way.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though my wife would probably describe it more clearly than I can.\n\nShe says sometimes I’m pacing around the house, moving papers from one place to another like I’m being driven by a motor. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and it’s like I’m slowed way down… she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me forever to answer.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but… yes, I think there’s been some of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d call it harder in a major way.\n\nI do find myself second-guessing things more, even small choices, but that may just be me being distracted or tired. I don’t want to overstate it. If you mean like being unable to make decisions at all, I don’t think it’s quite that… at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your physical activity level or movement since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my wife has noticed it more than I have.\n\nShe says I’ll pace around the house and keep rearranging papers, like I can’t settle down. And then other times I’ll just sit at the kitchen table and everything seems slowed down—she’ll ask me something simple and it takes me a long time to answer.\n\nSo… yes, I guess there have been changes. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure or interest in since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean… I still try to do things, because I know I’m supposed to.\n\nPainting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater—those used to kind of anchor me. Even sitting with patients, honestly, used to feel meaningful. Lately it all just feels… flat. Like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but… no, not much really feels pleasurable right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: Um… pretty much since Garrett passed, I think. At first I told myself it was just normal guilt, you know, going over things after someone dies.\n\nBut it’s been… persistent. I keep replaying every last conversation I had with him, and then it spreads to other things too—like patient sessions I cancelled. I start convincing myself I failed everyone somehow.\n\nI know that may sound excessive. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It just… keeps coming back, this sense that I’m in the way, and that people might be better off if I weren’t. Is that what you mean by worthlessness?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other unusual perceptions, such as seeing things that aren't there, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Um… yes. I’ve been a little embarrassed to say it that way, because I know how it sounds.\n\nIt’s not so much seeing things, exactly. It’s more that I hear Garrett’s voice. Sometimes it’s as clear as if he’s right behind me—like when I’m out on the trail, or in my painting room. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these intense yearnings or longing for Garrett for more than a few months?\n\nPatient: Not more than a few months, no.
